It’s more like I’m watching the situation happen and knowing what my reaction ought to be, but I can’t quite access it.\n\nSo if you mean have there been stretches where I felt genuinely myself again between low periods — no, not really. There might be less bad moments, or quieter moments, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where the warmth or enjoyment really came back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where your low mood or loss of interest seemed to lift or improve since the accident?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in the way I think you mean.\n\nThere are brief times when I’m less weighed down, I guess. Like if my wife is trying to get us back into one of our old Sunday routines — the coffee, the walk, the things we used to do without even thinking about it — I can tell part of me wants to respond to it. I know it should feel nice. I know I used to feel close to her in those moments, like it was this small thing that made life feel steady and good.\n\nBut now it’s like the mood might quiet down a bit, but the real happiness doesn’t come back. The closeness doesn’t really land. I’m sitting there with her, doing the same things, and I’m aware that this is supposed to be comforting, but I don’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nSame with work. If something goes well, I can recognize it logically — like, “Okay, that was a good outcome, that should be satisfying.” And maybe for a minute I feel slightly relieved. But it doesn’t lift into anything real. It doesn’t feel like enjoyment or pride or whatever I would’ve felt before.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there have been brief improvements, maybe there are moments where it’s not as intense. But I don’t feel like the happiness or interest has actually come back, even briefly. Not in a way that feels real.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two weeks where you felt extremely depressed or down, distinct from the flatness you've described?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d separate it that cleanly, honestly.\n\nSince the accident, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt very low, but when you say “at least two weeks” and “distinct from the flatness,” I have a hard time drawing a line around it. It hasn’t felt like clear episodes to me, like two weeks where one thing started and then ended and then I returned to some other baseline.\n\nIt’s more like the flatness is just… there, and sometimes the heaviness is worse on top of it. Some days or stretches feel darker, if that’s the right word, but I don’t know that I could confidently say, “Yes, that was a separate two-week period of extreme depression.” I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve felt down, but I’m not sure about a distinct two-week episode like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months since the accident where you felt consistently better, closer to your normal self?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think so. Not for two months.\n\nHonestly, it’s been going on since the retrofit accident, and that was about a year and seven months ago now. I almost hate saying it that way because it makes it sound very neat and measurable, like I’ve been keeping a calendar of it, but when I look back, I can’t find a real stretch where I’d say, “There, that was when I was basically myself again.”\n\nThere have been days that were a little less bad, or weeks where I was functioning more smoothly on the outside, maybe. But consistently better, closer to normal, for a couple of months? No. Since the accident, it’s felt like this has just stayed with me in one form or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're back to your normal self at any point since the accident?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt back to normal at any point.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been moments where I’m functioning, or where from the outside I probably look more like myself. I can get through a day, answer people, do what I’m supposed to do. But if you mean actually feeling like me again — like before the retrofit accident — no. I can’t point to a time where I’d say, “Yes, that’s when I came back.”\n\nIt’s been… what, about a year and seven months now since that happened, and it feels like this has been threaded through all of it. Not always exactly the same every day, but I don’t think there’s been a point where I felt like my normal self again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable, and you weren't able to sleep because you didn't feel tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like that.\n\nI’ve had nights where I couldn’t sleep, but not because I felt great or full of energy or like I didn’t need sleep. And I don’t really have periods where I’m extremely happy or wound up in that way. Irritable, maybe in the ordinary sense sometimes, but not like a whole period where I’m energized and not tired.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had anything like what you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms, such as the flatness and loss of interest, seemed to improve for at least two months before returning?\n\nPatient: No, not for two months. I keep coming back to that because I’ve tried to think of it in blocks of time, like maybe there was a better stretch I’m forgetting, but I really don’t think there was.\n\nSince the retrofit accident — about a year and seven months ago now — it’s just been there. Maybe it shifts a little, maybe some days are easier to get through than others, but I can’t honestly say there was a two-month period where the flatness or not caring about things really lifted and then came back. It’s more like it’s been continuous since then, just varying a bit in intensity.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted without significant improvement for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been going on since the retrofit accident, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nI mean, it feels long enough that I sometimes almost round it up in my head, because it’s been such a big stretch of time, but if we’re being exact, it hasn’t been a full two years. It’s been pretty much from then until now, without what I’d call a significant improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for most of the day, nearly every day, since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, since the accident, yes, it’s been there most of the time. The retrofit accident was about a year and seven months ago, and from then on it’s felt like this has just sort of stayed with me day after day.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like every single hour is exactly the same, because it’s not always identical, but if you’re asking whether it’s been present most days, for most of the day, since then… yeah, that would be fair to say. It hasn’t really gone away in any meaningful stretch since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not at least two years. I know it maybe sounds like I’m splitting hairs, but the timing matters, right? It’s been since the retrofit accident, and that was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nSo… it’s a long time, and it feels longer than that in my head sometimes, because it’s been such a constant part of everything since then. I’ve had periods before where I was worn down or unhappy for a while, like if work was going badly or there was tension at home, but it wasn’t like this thing where I lost the ability to feel interested in life, and then it lifted, and then came back again later.\n\nBefore the accident, if I was in a bad mood or stressed, it usually made sense to me. There was a reason, and once the reason eased up, I felt like myself again. I could enjoy things again without having to think about it. Comfortable. Like I was with her, actually with her. Now she’ll try to get us back into that, and I can tell she’s hoping it’ll feel familiar or nice, and I go along with it because I want to. I don’t want her to think I don’t care. But inside it just feels… flat. I’m there, but the warmth isn’t there.\n\nAnd it’s not only at home. At work, if something goes well, I can recognize that it went well. I can say the right thing, tell the team good job, understand that I should feel satisfied or proud or relieved. But I don’t really feel it. It’s like the part where pleasure or happiness is supposed to kick in just doesn’t happen. So, no, I don’t really enjoy things the way I used to before the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Or maybe it’s not exactly that I’ve lost interest in the idea of them, because part of me still wants to want them, if that makes sense. I’ll still agree to do things, especially with my wife, because I remember that they used to matter to me.\n\nLike those Sunday routines I mentioned — we used to have this whole rhythm, and I knew when I was in it that I was happy. Not in some dramatic way, just… content, close to her, settled. Now she’ll try to recreate that, and I can see what she’s doing, and I want it to work. I really do. But I don’t feel the happiness or the closeness the way I used to. It’s like I’m watching myself participate in something that should feel good.\n\nSame with work. If something goes well, I can understand that it’s a good thing. I can recognize it, I can even say, “That went well,” but there’s no real satisfaction behind it. So yes, I guess I’ve lost interest, but the harder part is that even when I try to do the things anyway, the good feeling just doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, but it’s not always worry in the sense of sitting there thinking, “What if this or that happens?” Sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m constantly scanning.\n\nAt work, especially when I’m out on the floor, I’m checking everything — the gauges, where the exits are, where people’s hands are, where the emergency stops are. I’ll do it over and over, even if I already checked a minute before. Part of me knows I’m probably overdoing it, but it feels like if I stop paying attention, I’ll miss something important.\n\nEven in meetings, I catch myself choosing a seat where I can see the door. I don’t really like having my back to it. I’ll sit there trying to listen, but some part of me is still tracking who’s coming in, who’s moving around, what’s near me. So yes, I guess I do feel anxious or worried a lot, but it comes out as being on guard all the time more than just… worrying in words.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this kind of persistent low mood or loss of interest before the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches like anyone, I guess — stress at work, arguments at home, times where I was tired or discouraged — but I don’t remember having this kind of ongoing flatness or low mood before the accident.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because when you’re living your life at the time you don’t always label things, you know? But looking back, I still had things that pulled me out of myself. I could enjoy my routines, look forward to something, feel like myself again after a rough week. This feels different from that.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there were multiple periods like this before. Not that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this kind of low mood or loss of interest before the accident and then had a period where it went away, only to come back again?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Not in that pattern, anyway.\n\nI’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was something I’m forgetting. I’ve had periods before where I was worn down or unhappy for a while, like if work was going badly or there was tension at home, but it wasn’t like this thing where I lost the ability to feel interested in life, and then it lifted, and then came back again later.\n\nBefore the accident, if I was in a bad mood or stressed, it usually made sense to me. There was a reason, and once the reason eased up, I felt like myself again. I could enjoy things again without having to think about it. It didn’t feel like this… ongoing absence of feeling.\n\nSo no, I can’t really point to a time before where this same low mood or loss of interest happened, went away, and then returned. Not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, to be honest.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that — not that I’ve noticed. I mean, there may have been some normal up and down, because my routines haven’t been exactly the same since the accident, but I couldn’t give you a number, and I don’t think there’s been some obvious big change where people are commenting on it or I’m needing different sizes.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed any significant weight change, at least not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt significantly better, almost back to normal, between episodes of feeling low or uninterested?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it “back to normal,” no. That’s actually part of what bothers me so much.\n\nThere are moments where things should feel better, if that makes sense. Like my wife will try to get us back into our old Sunday routine — the kind of thing I used to like, nothing dramatic, just familiar and comfortable — and I can tell she’s trying to make it feel normal again. Or something will go well at work, and on paper I know I should feel relieved or pleased. I can recognize, intellectually, “Okay, that was good.”\n\nBut the feeling doesn’t really come with it. I don’t get that real happiness or closeness from it the way I used to. It’s more like I’m watching the situation happen and knowing what my reaction ought to be, but I can’t quite access it.\n\nSo if you mean have there been stretches where I felt genuinely myself again between low periods — no, not really. There might be less bad moments, or quieter moments, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where the warmth or enjoyment really came back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how brief, where your low mood or loss of interest seemed to lift or improve since the accident?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in the way I think you mean.\n\nThere are brief times when I’m less weighed down, I guess. Like if my wife is trying to get us back into one of our old Sunday routines — the coffee, the walk, the things we used to do without even thinking about it — I can tell part of me wants to respond to it. I know it should feel nice. I know I used to feel close to her in those moments, like it was this small thing that made life feel steady and good.\n\nBut now it’s like the mood might quiet down a bit, but the real happiness doesn’t come back. The closeness doesn’t really land. I’m sitting there with her, doing the same things, and I’m aware that this is supposed to be comforting, but I don’t feel it the way I used to.\n\nSame with work. If something goes well, I can recognize it logically — like, “Okay, that was a good outcome, that should be satisfying.” And maybe for a minute I feel slightly relieved. But it doesn’t lift into anything real. It doesn’t feel like enjoyment or pride or whatever I would’ve felt before.\n\nSo… if you’re asking whether there have been brief improvements, maybe there are moments where it’s not as intense. But I don’t feel like the happiness or interest has actually come back, even briefly. Not in a way that feels real.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of at least two weeks where you felt extremely depressed or down, distinct from the flatness you've described?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d separate it that cleanly, honestly.\n\nSince the accident, there have definitely been times where I’ve felt very low, but when you say “at least two weeks” and “distinct from the flatness,” I have a hard time drawing a line around it. It hasn’t felt like clear episodes to me, like two weeks where one thing started and then ended and then I returned to some other baseline.\n\nIt’s more like the flatness is just… there, and sometimes the heaviness is worse on top of it. Some days or stretches feel darker, if that’s the right word, but I don’t know that I could confidently say, “Yes, that was a separate two-week period of extreme depression.” I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’ve felt down, but I’m not sure about a distinct two-week episode like you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two months since the accident where you felt consistently better, closer to your normal self?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think so. Not for two months.\n\nHonestly, it’s been going on since the retrofit accident, and that was about a year and seven months ago now. I almost hate saying it that way because it makes it sound very neat and measurable, like I’ve been keeping a calendar of it, but when I look back, I can’t find a real stretch where I’d say, “There, that was when I was basically myself again.”\n\nThere have been days that were a little less bad, or weeks where I was functioning more smoothly on the outside, maybe. But consistently better, closer to normal, for a couple of months? No. Since the accident, it’s felt like this has just stayed with me in one form or another.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're back to your normal self at any point since the accident?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt back to normal at any point.\n\nI mean, maybe there have been moments where I’m functioning, or where from the outside I probably look more like myself. I can get through a day, answer people, do what I’m supposed to do. But if you mean actually feeling like me again — like before the retrofit accident — no. I can’t point to a time where I’d say, “Yes, that’s when I came back.”\n\nIt’s been… what, about a year and seven months now since that happened, and it feels like this has been threaded through all of it. Not always exactly the same every day, but I don’t think there’s been a point where I felt like my normal self again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt extremely happy, energetic, or irritable, and you weren't able to sleep because you didn't feel tired?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like that.\n\nI’ve had nights where I couldn’t sleep, but not because I felt great or full of energy or like I didn’t need sleep. And I don’t really have periods where I’m extremely happy or wound up in that way. Irritable, maybe in the ordinary sense sometimes, but not like a whole period where I’m energized and not tired.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had anything like what you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms, such as the flatness and loss of interest, seemed to improve for at least two months before returning?\n\nPatient: No, not for two months. I keep coming back to that because I’ve tried to think of it in blocks of time, like maybe there was a better stretch I’m forgetting, but I really don’t think there was.\n\nSince the retrofit accident — about a year and seven months ago now — it’s just been there. Maybe it shifts a little, maybe some days are easier to get through than others, but I can’t honestly say there was a two-month period where the flatness or not caring about things really lifted and then came back. It’s more like it’s been continuous since then, just varying a bit in intensity.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted without significant improvement for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Not quite two years, no. It’s been going on since the retrofit accident, which was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nI mean, it feels long enough that I sometimes almost round it up in my head, because it’s been such a big stretch of time, but if we’re being exact, it hasn’t been a full two years. It’s been pretty much from then until now, without what I’d call a significant improvement.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present for most of the day, nearly every day, since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, since the accident, yes, it’s been there most of the time. The retrofit accident was about a year and seven months ago, and from then on it’s felt like this has just sort of stayed with me day after day.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like every single hour is exactly the same, because it’s not always identical, but if you’re asking whether it’s been present most days, for most of the day, since then… yeah, that would be fair to say. It hasn’t really gone away in any meaningful stretch since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not at least two years. I know it maybe sounds like I’m splitting hairs, but the timing matters, right? It’s been since the retrofit accident, and that was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nSo… it’s a long time, and it feels longer than that in my head sometimes, because it’s been such a constant part of everything since then. But if you’re asking specifically whether it’s been two years or more, then no — not yet. It’s been roughly nineteen months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms lasted for at least two years now?\n\nPatient: No… not quite two years now. I know I’ve probably said the timing a few times, but it started after the retrofit accident, and that was about a year and seven months ago.\n\nSo it’s been a long stretch — long enough that it feels like it’s taken over a big chunk of my life — but if we’re being exact, it hasn’t reached the two-year mark yet. It’s been roughly nineteen months."
